========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi. Um, well, I came in because I just keep getting these nagging thoughts that I forgot something important. Like, I help my aunt with her medicine, and I'll suddenly think I left it out wrong or something. Which reminds me, she actually had to switch pharmacies recently because the one on Elm Street closed down. It's such a shame, the pharmacist there, Gary, was so sweet. He always gave out those little strawberry hard candies with the wrappers that look like actual strawberries? I haven't seen those in years anywhere else. 

But anyway, yeah, I get these thoughts about her medicine, or like... my classroom. I teach, and I'll worry I didn't set up the classroom materials safely. Even when I know I probably handled it fine and everything is put away, the doubt just keeps popping back into my head. It's so frustrating. It's kind of like last week, the kids were doing this whole papier-mâché project, so there was flour and paste literally everywhere, and... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, just... I'm here because of those nagging doubts. They just keep popping up even when I know I did things right.

Um, yeah, actually I do. Before I leave the house, or, you know, before I lock up my classroom for the day, I’ll go back over the same things a few times. Like checking my aunt's pill organizer, making sure the stove is off, or looking through my lesson plans.

Half the parents are emailing me because they're so confused, and I had to spend three days at this mandatory training seminar in August just to figure it out myself. They held it at this hotel right off the interstate, and the air conditioning in the conference room was completely busted. We were all just sitting there sweating in our professional clothes, drinking this terrible lukewarm coffee. It was just awful. 

But um... sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right, checking things. Yeah, I just have to keep checking those things, or sometimes I just stand there and silently replay everything I did in my head over and over. I just have to keep doing it until I finally feel calm enough to move on. It's really frustrating because doing all that makes me run late sometimes.

He's always been a little difficult, though. Last year, he planted these huge bushes right on the property line, and they keep growing over my fence. I've had to trim them back three times already this summer. I even bought those special ergonomic shears because my hands were getting so sore, but they didn't really help that much. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, right, the checking. Yeah, I just check or replay things in my head until I feel like it's fine. It's not like a strict pattern or anything, just until I can finally relax a little.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are any rules or rituals that you must follow in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't have any strict rules or rituals I have to follow, like, in my daily life. Just the checking stuff I mentioned. I mean, I do have a schedule, obviously, because of teaching. You have to have a schedule with kids. If you don't, they just run wild. Speaking of running wild, we had a substitute teacher last week for one of the other classes, Mr. Henderson's class, and apparently, she let them have extra recess because she didn't want to deal with the science lesson. The kids were just out there for an hour, and one of them actually managed to get a kickball stuck in the gutter of the main building. The janitor, Frank—who is lovely, by the way, he always remembers everyone's birthdays—he had to get this massive ladder out to get it down. And of course, the principal walked out right as he was up there wobbling around. It was a whole ordeal. \n\nBut um... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, rules or rituals. No, I don't really have anything like that. Um, well, it's not like it completely stops me from doing what I need to do. I mean, I'm still getting through work and taking care of my aunt and everything. But, you know, my mornings definitely take longer than they should. I end up arriving at school feeling all rushed and behind before the kids even get there. And my lesson prep... well, that's just been a struggle. 

Speaking of lesson prep, I was trying to get ahead on next week's science unit on the water cycle, and I wanted to do this really fun experiment with shaving cream and food coloring to show precipitation. But when I went to the supply closet, someone had taken all the blue food coloring. Just the blue! Who uses that much blue food coloring? I think it was Mrs. Davis from second grade, she's always doing these elaborate ocean dioramas. Last year, she had her kids bring in actual sand, and it got tracked all over the hallway. The custodial staff was not happy about that at all. I had to go to three different grocery stores after school just to find more blue food coloring, and the traffic on Route 9 was backed up for miles because of some minor fender bender near the overpass. 

But um... what were we talking about? Oh, right, if it interferes. Yeah, I mean, I still get things done, but it's just... things don't feel as smooth. Even my evening journaling, which usually helps me unwind, it just doesn't feel as restorative as it used to because my mind is still kind of stuck on everything.

And of course, the principal walked out right as he was up there wobbling around. It was a whole ordeal. \n\nBut um... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, rules or rituals. No, I don't really have anything like that. Just the checking until I feel calm, but no specific rules or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking behaviors interfere with your daily activities or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it's not like it completely stops me from doing what I need to do. I mean, I'm still getting through work and taking care of my aunt and everything. But, you know, my mornings definitely take longer than they should. I end up arriving at school feeling all rushed and behind before the kids even get there. And my lesson prep... well, that's just been a struggle. \n\nSpeaking of lesson prep, I was trying to get ahead on next week's science unit on the water cycle, and I wanted to do this really fun experiment with shaving cream and food coloring to show precipitation. But when I went to the supply closet, someone had taken all the blue food coloring. Just the blue! Who uses that much blue food coloring? I think it was Mrs. Davis from second grade, she's always doing these elaborate ocean dioramas. Last year, she had her kids bring in actual sand, and it got tracked all over the hallway. The custodial staff was not happy about that at all. I had to go to three different grocery stores after school just to find more blue food coloring, and the traffic on Route 9 was backed up for miles because of some minor fender bender near the overpass. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? Oh, right, if it interferes. Yeah, I mean, I still get things done, but it's just... things don't feel as smooth. Even my evening journaling, which usually helps me unwind, it just doesn't feel as restorative as it used to because my mind is still kind of stuck on everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 31 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I forgot something important, like leaving my aunt’s medicine out wrong or not setting up my classroom materials safely, and even when I know I probably handled it, the doubt keeps popping back into my head.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. That's actually exactly why I came in. I just keep getting these nagging thoughts that I forgot something really important. Like, I'll worry that I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or that I didn't set up my classroom materials safely for the kids. 

Which, speaking of my aunt's medicine, the pharmacy just switched to those new push-and-turn caps, and they are completely impossible to open. I mean, my aunt has arthritis, so she can't do it at all. I have to open all of them for her on Sunday nights and put them in one of those little plastic pill organizers with the days of the week on them. But the one we have is from like, 1998, and the little snap lids are half broken. I tried to buy her a new one at Walgreens last Tuesday, but they only had these giant neon ones, and she hates bright colors. She says they hurt her eyes. She’s very particular like that. She even made the mail carrier stop wearing his sunglasses when he comes to the door to drop off packages because she said the reflection was too much. He’s a really good sport about it, though. His name is Gary, and he actually just had a baby girl, so we got him a little card... 

Wait, what was I saying? Oh, the thoughts in my head. Right. Yeah, so even when I know I probably handled it—like I know I put the medicine away or put the scissors in the locked drawer at school—the doubt just keeps popping back into my head anyway. It just won't go away.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, exactly. They just force their way in, no matter what I'm doing. Like I was saying, it's just this constant nagging feeling that I forgot something really important. I could be right in the middle of grading spelling tests, and suddenly my brain is just screaming at me that I left my aunt's medicine out wrong, or that I didn't put the classroom scissors away safely and one of the kids is going to get hurt. 

Which is so silly, because I'm so careful with the scissors. I have this special locked drawer in my desk just for sharp things. My desk is actually one of those old, heavy wooden ones from the 1970s. They don't make them like that anymore. The drawers stick sometimes, especially when it's humid. Last spring, it rained for like two weeks straight, and I couldn't get the bottom drawer open at all. I had to call maintenance, and this guy came up with a crowbar. He was wearing this hilarious t-shirt with a cat on it that said "Purr-fectly Fine." I asked him where he got it because my aunt loves cats, even though she's allergic. She used to have this beautiful orange tabby named Marmalade when I was little, but she had to give him to my cousin because her asthma got so bad... 

Um... sorry, what was the question? Oh, the thoughts forcing their way in. Yeah. Even when I'm trying to focus on something else, and even when I know I probably handled it—like I know I locked that drawer or put the medicine away—the doubt just keeps popping back up. It's like my brain won't let it go.

Um, not handwashing or counting, really. But I definitely have to do certain things. Like, before I leave the house in the morning or my classroom at the end of the day, I’ll go back over the same things a few times. I have to check the pill organizer, my lesson plans, the stove...

He's this older guy who always wears these bright yellow suspenders, every single day. He swears electric stoves don't heat up right, which I guess is true if you're searing a steak, but I don't even eat red meat. I actually became a pescatarian back in college after watching this documentary. My roommate Jessica made me watch it. She was super into environmentalism—she even made her own deodorant out of baking soda and coconut oil. It smelled exactly like macaroons, but it left these awful grease stains on all her shirts. We had to go to the laundromat on 4th Street because our building's washer was broken, and she spent like twenty dollars in quarters just trying to soak the stains out with dish soap. The lady who worked there gave us free coffee, though, which was nice...

Oh, gosh, sorry, I'm rambling again. You were asking about physical actions. Right. So, yeah, I don't count or anything, but I just have to keep checking those things. It's really frustrating, because having to do all that is exactly why I end up running late sometimes.

I end up arriving at school feeling all rushed and behind before the kids even get there. But when I went to the supply closet, someone had taken all the blue food coloring. Just the blue! Who uses that much blue food coloring? I think it was Mrs. Davis from second grade, she's always doing these elaborate ocean dioramas. Last year, she had her kids bring in actual sand, and it got tracked all over the hallway. The custodial staff was not happy about that at all. I had to go to three different grocery stores after school just to find more blue food coloring, and the traffic on Route 9 was backed up for miles because of some minor fender bender near the overpass. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? Oh, right, if it interferes. Yeah, I mean, I still get things done, but it's just... things don't feel as smooth. Even my evening journaling, which usually helps me unwind, it just doesn't feel as restorative as it used to because my mind is still kind of stuck on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That's actually exactly why I came in. I just keep getting these nagging thoughts that I forgot something really important. Like, I'll worry that I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or that I didn't set up my classroom materials safely for the kids. \n\nWhich, speaking of my aunt's medicine, the pharmacy just switched to those new push-and-turn caps, and they are completely impossible to open. I mean, my aunt has arthritis, so she can't do it at all. I have to open all of them for her on Sunday nights and put them in one of those little plastic pill organizers with the days of the week on them. It just won't go away.\n\nDoctor: Do these nagging thoughts feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. They just force their way in, no matter what I'm doing. Like I was saying, it's just this constant nagging feeling that I forgot something really important. I could be right in the middle of grading spelling tests, and suddenly my brain is just screaming at me that I left my aunt's medicine out wrong, or that I didn't put the classroom scissors away safely and one of the kids is going to get hurt. \n\nWhich is so silly, because I'm so careful with the scissors. I have this special locked drawer in my desk just for sharp things. My desk is actually one of those old, heavy wooden ones from the 1970s. They don't make them like that anymore. I had to call maintenance, and this guy came up with a crowbar. Yeah, completely unwanted. I never try to think about them, they just... happen. Like I said, it's just this nagging feeling that I forgot something important, like the medicine or the classroom setup. I don't want to be thinking about it at all. 

Actually, I'd much rather be thinking about my lesson plans. We're doing a unit on the solar system next week, which is my favorite. I spent all weekend cutting out these little foam planets. I had to go to three different craft stores just to find the right shade of blue for Neptune. The first store only had this weird teal color, and the cashier—this teenager who looked like he hadn't slept in a week—tried to tell me it was close enough. But kids are so smart, you know? If Neptune is teal, they're going to ask questions. My nephew, Leo, he's seven, and he already knows all the dwarf planets. He corrected my brother at Thanksgiving dinner about Makemake. My brother was trying to carve the turkey—which was incredibly dry, by the way, because my sister-in-law insists on baking it in this weird roasting bag she saw on Pinterest—and Leo just chimes in with all these astronomy facts. It was so cute, but my sister-in-law got really annoyed because she thought he was being disrespectful...

Wait, sorry. The thoughts. Right. Yeah, they just pop in. Even when I know I probably handled everything fine, the doubt just sneaks in completely on its own, and I can't stop it.

He always gave out those little strawberry hard candies with the wrappers that look like actual strawberries? I haven't seen those in years anywhere else. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I get these thoughts about her medicine, or like... my classroom. I teach, and I'll worry I didn't set up the classroom materials safely. Even when I know I probably handled it fine and everything is put away, the doubt just keeps popping back into my head. It's so frustrating. It's kind of like last week, the kids were doing this whole papier-mâché project, so there was flour and paste literally everywhere, and... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, just... I'm here because of those nagging doubts. They just keep popping up even when I know I did things right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to do something over and over, like checking on something, to make these nagging thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, actually I do. Before I leave the house, or, you know, before I lock up my classroom for the day, I’ll go back over the same things a few times. Like checking my aunt's pill organizer, making sure the stove is off, or looking through my lesson plans. \n\nWhich, honestly, the lesson plans are a whole other headache right now because the district just changed our math curriculum again. It's that new method where they want the kids to draw all these little boxes for multiplication? Half the parents are emailing me because they're so confused, and I had to spend three days at this mandatory training seminar in August just to figure it out myself. They held it at this hotel right off the interstate, and the air conditioning in the conference room was completely busted. We were all just sitting there sweating in our professional clothes, drinking this terrible lukewarm coffee. It was just awful. \n\nBut um... sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right, checking things. Yeah, I just have to keep checking those things, or sometimes I just stand there and silently replay everything I did in my head over and over. I just have to keep doing it until I finally feel calm enough to move on. It's really frustrating because doing all that makes me run late sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or replays in a very specific way to feel calm?\n\nPatient: Um, not exactly a specific way, no. I just kind of do it until I feel okay. Like, I don't have a set number of times or a routine, it's just... until the nagging feeling stops. Which reminds me, speaking of routines, my morning routine has been completely thrown off this week because my neighbor decided to start remodeling his kitchen. He's got these contractors showing up at 6:00 AM every single day, and they just start hammering away. I tried talking to him about it, but he just said they're on a tight schedule. He's always been a little difficult, though. Last year, he planted these huge bushes right on the property line, and they keep growing over my fence. I've had to trim them back three times already this summer. I even bought those special ergonomic shears because my hands were getting so sore, but they didn't really help that much. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, right, the checking. Yeah, I just check or replay things in my head until I feel like it's fine. It's not like a strict pattern or anything, just until I can finally relax a little.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are any rules or rituals that you must follow in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't have any strict rules or rituals I have to follow, like, in my daily life. Just the checking stuff I mentioned. I mean, I do have a schedule, obviously, because of teaching. You have to have a schedule with kids. If you don't, they just run wild. Speaking of running wild, we had a substitute teacher last week for one of the other classes, Mr. Henderson's class, and apparently, she let them have extra recess because she didn't want to deal with the science lesson. The kids were just out there for an hour, and one of them actually managed to get a kickball stuck in the gutter of the main building. The janitor, Frank—who is lovely, by the way, he always remembers everyone's birthdays—he had to get this massive ladder out to get it down. And of course, the principal walked out right as he was up there wobbling around. It was a whole ordeal. \n\nBut um... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, rules or rituals. No, I don't really have anything like that. Just the checking until I feel calm, but no specific rules or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking behaviors interfere with your daily activities or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it's not like it completely stops me from doing what I need to do. I mean, I'm still getting through work and taking care of my aunt and everything. But, you know, my mornings definitely take longer than they should. I end up arriving at school feeling all rushed and behind before the kids even get there. And my lesson prep... well, that's just been a struggle. \n\nSpeaking of lesson prep, I was trying to get ahead on next week's science unit on the water cycle, and I wanted to do this really fun experiment with shaving cream and food coloring to show precipitation. But when I went to the supply closet, someone had taken all the blue food coloring. Just the blue! Who uses that much blue food coloring? I think it was Mrs. Davis from second grade, she's always doing these elaborate ocean dioramas. Last year, she had her kids bring in actual sand, and it got tracked all over the hallway. The custodial staff was not happy about that at all. I had to go to three different grocery stores after school just to find more blue food coloring, and the traffic on Route 9 was backed up for miles because of some minor fender bender near the overpass. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? Oh, right, if it interferes. Yeah, I mean, I still get things done, but it's just... things don't feel as smooth. Even my evening journaling, which usually helps me unwind, it just doesn't feel as restorative as it used to because my mind is still kind of stuck on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That's actually exactly why I came in. I just keep getting these nagging thoughts that I forgot something really important. Like, I'll worry that I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or that I didn't set up my classroom materials safely for the kids. \n\nWhich, speaking of my aunt's medicine, the pharmacy just switched to those new push-and-turn caps, and they are completely impossible to open. I mean, my aunt has arthritis, so she can't do it at all. I have to open all of them for her on Sunday nights and put them in one of those little plastic pill organizers with the days of the week on them. But the one we have is from like, 1998, and the little snap lids are half broken. I tried to buy her a new one at Walgreens last Tuesday, but they only had these giant neon ones, and she hates bright colors. She says they hurt her eyes. She’s very particular like that. She even made the mail carrier stop wearing his sunglasses when he comes to the door to drop off packages because she said the reflection was too much. He’s a really good sport about it, though. His name is Gary, and he actually just had a baby girl, so we got him a little card... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, the thoughts in my head. Right. Yeah, so even when I know I probably handled it—like I know I put the medicine away or put the scissors in the locked drawer at school—the doubt just keeps popping back into my head anyway. It just won't go away.\n\nDoctor: Do these nagging thoughts feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. They just force their way in, no matter what I'm doing. Like I was saying, it's just this constant nagging feeling that I forgot something really important. I could be right in the middle of grading spelling tests, and suddenly my brain is just screaming at me that I left my aunt's medicine out wrong, or that I didn't put the classroom scissors away safely and one of the kids is going to get hurt. \n\nWhich is so silly, because I'm so careful with the scissors. I have this special locked drawer in my desk just for sharp things. My desk is actually one of those old, heavy wooden ones from the 1970s. They don't make them like that anymore. The drawers stick sometimes, especially when it's humid. Last spring, it rained for like two weeks straight, and I couldn't get the bottom drawer open at all. I had to call maintenance, and this guy came up with a crowbar. He was wearing this hilarious t-shirt with a cat on it that said \"Purr-fectly Fine.\" I asked him where he got it because my aunt loves cats, even though she's allergic. She used to have this beautiful orange tabby named Marmalade when I was little, but she had to give him to my cousin because her asthma got so bad... \n\nUm... sorry, what was the question? Oh, the thoughts forcing their way in. Yeah. Even when I'm trying to focus on something else, and even when I know I probably handled it—like I know I locked that drawer or put the medicine away—the doubt just keeps popping back up. It's like my brain won't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific physical actions, like handwashing or counting, in response to these nagging thoughts?\n\nPatient: Um, not handwashing or counting, really. But I definitely have to do certain things. Like, before I leave the house in the morning or my classroom at the end of the day, I’ll go back over the same things a few times. I have to check the pill organizer, my lesson plans, the stove... \n\nThe stove especially makes me nervous because it's gas. My landlord, Mr. Henderson, refuses to upgrade it to electric. He's this older guy who always wears these bright yellow suspenders, every single day. He swears electric stoves don't heat up right, which I guess is true if you're searing a steak, but I don't even eat red meat. I actually became a pescatarian back in college after watching this documentary. My roommate Jessica made me watch it. She was super into environmentalism—she even made her own deodorant out of baking soda and coconut oil. It smelled exactly like macaroons, but it left these awful grease stains on all her shirts. We had to go to the laundromat on 4th Street because our building's washer was broken, and she spent like twenty dollars in quarters just trying to soak the stains out with dish soap. The lady who worked there gave us free coffee, though, which was nice...\n\nOh, gosh, sorry, I'm rambling again. You were asking about physical actions. Right. So, yeah, I don't count or anything, but I just have to keep checking those things. Or sometimes I just stand there and silently replay what I did in my head, over and over, until I finally feel calm enough to just move on. It's really frustrating, because having to do all that is exactly why I end up running late sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these nagging thoughts are unwanted and pop into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, completely unwanted. I never try to think about them, they just... happen. Like I said, it's just this nagging feeling that I forgot something important, like the medicine or the classroom setup. I don't want to be thinking about it at all. \n\nActually, I'd much rather be thinking about my lesson plans. We're doing a unit on the solar system next week, which is my favorite. I spent all weekend cutting out these little foam planets. I had to go to three different craft stores just to find the right shade of blue for Neptune. The first store only had this weird teal color, and the cashier—this teenager who looked like he hadn't slept in a week—tried to tell me it was close enough. But kids are so smart, you know? If Neptune is teal, they're going to ask questions. My nephew, Leo, he's seven, and he already knows all the dwarf planets. He corrected my brother at Thanksgiving dinner about Makemake. My brother was trying to carve the turkey—which was incredibly dry, by the way, because my sister-in-law insists on baking it in this weird roasting bag she saw on Pinterest—and Leo just chimes in with all these astronomy facts. It was so cute, but my sister-in-law got really annoyed because she thought he was being disrespectful...\n\nWait, sorry. The thoughts. Right. Yeah, they just pop in. Even when I know I probably handled everything fine, the doubt just sneaks in completely on its own, and I can't stop it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like checking my aunt's pill organizer, making sure the stove is off, or looking through my lesson plans. \n\nWhich, honestly, the lesson plans are a whole other headache right now because the district just changed our math curriculum again. It's that new method where they want the kids to draw all these little boxes for multiplication? Half the parents are emailing me because they're so confused, and I had to spend three days at this mandatory training seminar in August just to figure it out myself. They held it at this hotel right off the interstate, and the air conditioning in the conference room was completely busted. We were all just sitting there sweating in our professional clothes, drinking this terrible lukewarm coffee. It was just awful. \n\nBut um... sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right, checking things. Yeah, I just have to keep checking those things, or sometimes I just stand there and silently replay everything I did in my head over and over. I just have to keep doing it until I finally feel calm enough to move on. It's really frustrating because doing all that makes me run late sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or replays in a very specific way to feel calm?\n\nPatient: Um, not exactly a specific way, no. I just kind of do it until I feel okay. Like, I don't have a set number of times or a routine, it's just... until the nagging feeling stops. Which reminds me, speaking of routines, my morning routine has been completely thrown off this week because my neighbor decided to start remodeling his kitchen. He's got these contractors showing up at 6:00 AM every single day, and they just start hammering away. I tried talking to him about it, but he just said they're on a tight schedule. He's always been a little difficult, though. Last year, he planted these huge bushes right on the property line, and they keep growing over my fence. I've had to trim them back three times already this summer. I even bought those special ergonomic shears because my hands were getting so sore, but they didn't really help that much. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, right, the checking. Yeah, I just check or replay things in my head until I feel like it's fine. It's not like a strict pattern or anything, just until I can finally relax a little.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are any rules or rituals that you must follow in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. Speaking of running wild, we had a substitute teacher last week for one of the other classes, Mr. Henderson's class, and apparently, she let them have extra recess because she didn't want to deal with the science lesson. The kids were just out there for an hour, and one of them actually managed to get a kickball stuck in the gutter of the main building. The janitor, Frank—who is lovely, by the way, he always remembers everyone's birthdays—he had to get this massive ladder out to get it down. And of course, the principal walked out right as he was up there wobbling around. It was a whole ordeal. \n\nBut um... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, rules or rituals. No, I don't really have anything like that. I mean, I'm still getting through work and taking care of my aunt and everything. But, you know, my mornings definitely take longer than they should. I end up arriving at school feeling all rushed and behind before the kids even get there. But when I went to the supply closet, someone had taken all the blue food coloring. Just the blue! Who uses that much blue food coloring? I think it was Mrs. Davis from second grade, she's always doing these elaborate ocean dioramas. Last year, she had her kids bring in actual sand, and it got tracked all over the hallway. The custodial staff was not happy about that at all. I had to go to three different grocery stores after school just to find more blue food coloring, and the traffic on Route 9 was backed up for miles because of some minor fender bender near the overpass. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? Oh, right, if it interferes. Yeah, I mean, I still get things done, but it's just... things don't feel as smooth. Even my evening journaling, which usually helps me unwind, it just doesn't feel as restorative as it used to because my mind is still kind of stuck on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I have this special locked drawer in my desk just for sharp things. My desk is actually one of those old, heavy wooden ones from the 1970s. They don't make them like that anymore. Yeah, definitely against my will. I think that's why I finally came in, honestly. I just keep getting these nagging thoughts that I forgot something important, like leaving my aunt’s medicine out wrong or not setting up my classroom materials safely.

She bought it at a garage sale from this couple who was retiring to Florida. I don't know how anyone can live in Florida, honestly, the humidity is just unbearable. I went to Orlando a few years ago for an educator's conference, and my hair was just a giant ball of frizz the entire week. We stayed at this resort that had a lazy river, which sounds fun, but the water was suspiciously warm and there were all these screaming toddlers with water guns. My friend Sarah—she teaches fourth grade—refused to even go near the pool area. She's really strict about germs, which makes sense because schools are basically giant petri dishes. Just last week half my class was out with this awful stomach bug, and the custodian had to come in with this special industrial spray...

Yeah, I do. I feel like I absolutely have to do something or the feeling just won't go away. Before I leave the house or my classroom, I'll go back over the same things a few times—checking my aunt's pill organizer, making sure the stove is off, looking over my lesson plans... or sometimes I just silently replay what I did in my head until I feel calm enough to move on. 

It definitely makes me run late sometimes, which is so frustrating because I really hate being late. My mom was always late to everything when I was growing up. The Sunday one in the Times is always the best, though last week there was this clue about a 1920s jazz musician that I just could not figure out...

Wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The actions. Yeah, I just have to keep checking things or replaying it all in my head until that nagging feeling finally settles down.

Um, yeah, I do. Like I was saying, I'll go back over things—the pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans—or I'll just keep replaying it all in my head until I finally feel calm enough to move on. It's the only way I can get that relief, even though it makes me run late sometimes.

It smells amazing, like a forest, but I have to be careful because last winter a moth got in there and completely ruined my favorite wool sweater. Oh, goodness, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. But yes, to answer your question, checking those things or replaying them in my head does give me relief. I just have to do it until I feel calm.

Well, I wouldn't say significantly interfering, no. I mean, I’m still getting through all my work and family commitments just fine. But my mornings definitely take a lot longer now, and I sometimes arrive at school feeling so rushed or behind before the day even starts. And then at night, my lesson prep and my evening journaling just don’t feel as smooth or restorative as they used to. 

I’ve always loved journaling, it's usually my favorite part of the day. I have this massive collection of dotted notebooks. I only use the dotted ones, never the lined ones, because the lines feel too restrictive, you know? I buy them from this little stationery shop downtown, right next to that bakery that sells the giant almond croissants. Oh my gosh, their croissants are incredible. The baker is this older guy who moved here from France, and he always gives me a free cookie if I come in right before closing. I tried baking bread myself a couple of years ago, but my sourdough starter completely died after like three days. I named it Dough-rthy, like Dorothy from the Wizard of Oz. Did you know they used Jell-O crystals to color the horses in Emerald City in that movie? I read that online and I just couldn't believe it, because wouldn't the horses try to lick it off? I mean, my aunt's dog will lick literally anything with a flavor, especially if it's sweet...

Oh, wow, I'm doing it again. I completely lost the thread. I'm so sorry. But yeah, to answer your question, it's not ruining my relationships or stopping me from working or anything. It just makes my mornings way more stressful and kind of takes the relaxation out of my journaling and lesson planning.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, distress is a strong word, but yeah, it's definitely frustrating. Like I was saying a minute ago, I'm still getting through all my work and family stuff just fine. But my mornings just take so much longer now because I have to do all my checking, and I end up getting to school feeling completely rushed and already behind before the bell even rings. And then in the evenings, doing my lesson prep and my journaling just doesn't feel as relaxing or restorative as it used to. 

It's a shame about the journaling, really, because I used to look forward to it all day. I have this specific pen I like to use, a pilot G2 in navy blue, not black. Black ink always feels so harsh to me, don't you think? Navy blue is softer. I actually painted my bedroom a navy blue a few years ago. The color was called "Midnight Sea" or something like that. I bought the paint at this hardware store where the owner has a parrot that just sits by the register. It's a macaw, I think? Huge beak. It actually squawked at me when I was buying the paint rollers, and I jumped so high I dropped my keys. My keys have this really bulky keychain on them from when I went to the Grand Canyon in college. We drove the whole way there in my friend Sarah's beat-up Honda Civic, and the air conditioning broke somewhere in New Mexico. We had to roll the windows down, and the wind was so loud we couldn't even hear the radio. We ended up singing show tunes at the top of our lungs to pass the time. Sarah was a theater major, so she knew every word to Les Misérables... 

Wait, I'm sorry, I drifted off again. But yeah, it's not ruining my life, but it's definitely causing some stress because my mornings are so rushed and my evenings just aren't as peaceful anymore.

Yeah, exactly. Like I was saying earlier, I really feel like I *have* to do it. Well, like I was just saying, I do go back over the same things a few times before I leave the house or my classroom. The stove, the pill organizer, my lesson plans, or just silently replaying what I did in my head. I just have to do it until I feel calm enough to move on, even though it makes me run late. 

As for something bad happening... I guess I just can't shake the feeling that I missed something important if I don't check. Like with the stove, I just need that relief. But speaking of the stove and fires, we actually had a fire drill at school on Tuesday right in the middle of our math block. It completely derailed my fractions lesson. The kids get so incredibly wound up during drills, it takes twenty minutes just to get them to stand in a straight line. Little Leo, one of my students, actually lost his shoe in the woodchips on the playground while we were waiting for the all-clear. I really don't understand why schools still use woodchips, honestly. They're basically just splinter traps. I got a massive splinter last summer from this wooden patio chair at a seafood restaurant down by the lake. The waiter had to bring me a pair of tweezers from their first aid kit. The food was really good, though—they had these amazing crab cakes with a spicy remoulade sauce. I tried making remoulade at home once, but I put way too much paprika in it and it turned bright red, almost like ketchup... 

Oh, gosh, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry, my mind just completely wandered off. But yes, to answer your question, I don't know if it's always a specific fear of a disaster, but I just have to check those things over and over until I feel calm, otherwise I just can't move on with my day.

Even when I know I probably handled everything fine, the doubt just sneaks in completely on its own, and I can't stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these nagging thoughts and checking behaviors are affecting your relationships with others or your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Um, I mean, I'm still getting through everything okay. Like my work and family commitments are fine. But my mornings definitely take a lot longer now because of all the checking, so I sometimes arrive at school feeling completely rushed and behind before the day even starts. \n\nWhich is the absolute worst feeling, especially because our staff parking lot is a total nightmare if you don't get there by 7:15. The PE teacher, Mr. Davis, drives this massive silver truck, and he always parks right on the yellow line. He has this bumper sticker that says 'I'd rather be fly fishing,' which I just don't get. Fishing seems so boring, you know? Just sitting out on a lake for hours. My uncle used to go ice fishing up in Minnesota, and one time he accidentally dropped his favorite thermos right down the ice hole. It was this vintage plaid Stanley thermos that belonged to my grandpa, and my aunt was furious. She wouldn't let him buy a new one for like a year, so he had to drink his coffee out of these flimsy styrofoam cups from the gas station down the street from their house. The guy who worked there was super friendly though, he always gave my uncle free powdered donuts... \n\nOh, sorry! Quality of life. Right. Um, so yeah, the mornings are really rushed. And then by the time I get home, things like my lesson prep and my evening journaling just don't feel as smooth or restorative as they used to. It's like my brain is just kind of drained from dealing with the nagging feeling all day, so even my quiet time doesn't feel as relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are entering your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely against my will. I think that's why I finally came in, honestly. I just keep getting these nagging thoughts that I forgot something important, like leaving my aunt’s medicine out wrong or not setting up my classroom materials safely. \n\nMy aunt takes a lot of medication, and she keeps it all in this massive plastic container that I'm pretty sure is actually a fishing tackle box. She bought it at a garage sale from this couple who was retiring to Florida. I don't know how anyone can live in Florida, honestly, the humidity is just unbearable. I went to Orlando a few years ago for an educator's conference, and my hair was just a giant ball of frizz the entire week. We stayed at this resort that had a lazy river, which sounds fun, but the water was suspiciously warm and there were all these screaming toddlers with water guns. My friend Sarah—she teaches fourth grade—refused to even go near the pool area. She's really strict about germs, which makes sense because schools are basically giant petri dishes. Just last week half my class was out with this awful stomach bug, and the custodian had to come in with this special industrial spray... \n\nOh, wait. Sorry. I completely lost track again. You asked if the thoughts were against my will. But yeah, even when I know I probably handled the medicine or the classroom stuff perfectly fine, the doubt just keeps popping back into my head completely on its own. I can't keep it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must take specific actions when these nagging thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I feel like I absolutely have to do something or the feeling just won't go away. Before I leave the house or my classroom, I'll go back over the same things a few times—checking my aunt's pill organizer, making sure the stove is off, looking over my lesson plans... or sometimes I just silently replay what I did in my head until I feel calm enough to move on. \n\nIt definitely makes me run late sometimes, which is so frustrating because I really hate being late. My mom was always late to everything when I was growing up. I remember one time she was an hour late picking me up from soccer practice, and it was starting to rain, and the coach—this guy named Mr. Henderson who always wore these neon yellow windbreakers—had to wait with me in his car. His car smelled exactly like wet dog and old french fries. He had this golden retriever named Buster who apparently loved going through drive-thrus. I actually love golden retrievers, they're so sweet. My neighbor has one that always brings me the newspaper in the morning, though half the time it's completely covered in drool, so I have to dry it off on the radiator before I can even read the crossword puzzle. I love doing the crossword, it's like a little brain workout, you know? The Sunday one in the Times is always the best, though last week there was this clue about a 1920s jazz musician that I just could not figure out...\n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The actions. Yeah, I just have to keep checking things or replaying it all in my head until that nagging feeling finally settles down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel relieved of anxiety when you complete these checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, I do. Like I was saying, I'll go back over things—the pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans—or I'll just keep replaying it all in my head until I finally feel calm enough to move on. It's the only way I can get that relief, even though it makes me run late sometimes. \n\nRunning late is just the worst, especially on Tuesdays. Tuesdays are when they serve those square pizzas in the cafeteria, and the kids get so wound up. I swear, it's like the cheese has sugar in it or something. One time, little Tommy—he's this sweet kid but he has absolutely no indoor voice—he dropped his entire tray of square pizza right in the middle of the hallway. And of course, it landed face down. It always lands face down, doesn't it? Just like toast with jelly. My grandma used to make the best homemade strawberry jelly. She grew the strawberries in her own backyard in these big wooden planter boxes that my grandpa built for her. He was a carpenter, and he built almost all the furniture in their house. He even built this beautiful cedar chest that sits at the foot of my bed now. It smells amazing, like a forest, but I have to be careful because last winter a moth got in there and completely ruined my favorite wool sweater. It was this gorgeous mustard yellow color that I bought at a little boutique when I went to Chicago for my cousin's wedding. The wedding was at this botanical garden, and it was beautiful, but the mosquitos were just enormous...\n\nOh, goodness, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. But yes, to answer your question, checking those things or replaying them in my head does give me relief. I just have to do it until I feel calm.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these nagging thoughts and checking behaviors are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy your daily activities or maintain relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say significantly interfering, no. We drove the whole way there in my friend Sarah's beat-up Honda Civic, and the air conditioning broke somewhere in New Mexico.
